#2956. Ravens updated QB depth chart after Jacoby Brissett trade

The Baltimore Ravens have a new starting quarterback, and the team's depth chart has changed again after officially acquiring Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals.

Baltimore finalized the trade Friday, receiving Brissett and a 2027 fifth-round draft pick in exchange for a 2027 fourth-round selection and a 2028 seventh-round pick. The Ravens subsequently waived quarterback Austin Reed, who served as Joe Fagnano's backup during Thursday night's 33-10 loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars.

Brissett is expected to become Baltimore's fourth different starting quarterback in four consecutive games when the Ravens host the Los Angeles Chargers on Monday, Nov. 16.

Here's how Baltimore's quarterback depth chart projects following the Brissett trade and Reed's release:

Jacoby Brissett — QB1: Expected starter against the Chargers.

Joe Fagnano — QB2: Undrafted rookie who started Thursday against Jacksonville.

Kedon Slovis — QB3: Acquired from Green Bay's practice squad earlier this week.

Lamar Jackson — Injured reserve: Recovering from a right thumb injury that required surgery.

Tyler Huntley — Injured reserve: Dealing with a toe injury reportedly expected to end his season.

The backup order behind Brissett is projected and has not been formally announced by Minter.

Brissett immediately becomes Baltimore's most experienced available quarterback, bringing 73 career starts over 11 NFL seasons. The 33-year-old started Arizona's first eight games this season, completing 63.3% of his passes for 1,719 yards, 10 touchdowns and seven interceptions before losing his starting job.

His experience should provide stability for an offense that has struggled to produce consistently without Jackson.

Baltimore has been held below 14 points in four consecutive games for the first time in franchise history, and Thursday's loss illustrated the limitations of its inexperienced quarterback room. Brissett will have more than a week to become familiar with Doyle's offense and establish timing with Zay Flowers, Rashod Bateman and Mark Andrews.

Fagnano started Thursday against Jacksonville but struggled throughout the 33-10 defeat, completing 10 of 18 passes for 60 yards and two interceptions. He finished with a 22.7 passer rating as Baltimore managed just 55 net passing yards.

The former UConn quarterback remains an option behind Brissett, though the coaching staff must decide whether his familiarity with the offense gives him an edge over Slovis. Baltimore signed Slovis from Green Bay's practice squad Nov. 3, adding another quarterback after Jackson and Huntley suffered injuries against Buffalo.

Reed's departure further clarifies the quarterback rotation. He was promoted from the practice squad Thursday and served as Fagnano's backup against Jacksonville but did not play.

Jackson and Huntley were placed on injured reserve Thursday, making both quarterbacks ineligible to return for at least four games. Jackson's recovery timeline remains uncertain following his thumb injury. Baltimore has not ruled out a potential return later this season, although there is no guarantee the two-time NFL MVP will play again in 2026.

Huntley's injury is reportedly season-ending, leaving Brissett, Fagnano and Slovis as Baltimore's available quarterbacks.

The Ravens enter Week 10 at 4-5, riding a four-game losing streak. Brissett's arrival won't automatically fix an inconsistent offensive line or Baltimore's problems sustaining drives, but it gives the organization a veteran quarterback who can run the offense as the team tries to keep its playoff hopes alive.

#2955. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2952. Gaël Monfils stays in tennis as captain of France's Davis Cup team

PARIS (AP) — Gaël Monfils will stay in tennis as captain of France's Davis Cup team, the national federation said Saturday.

#2951. Sen. Chris Murphy released from hospital after stroke, will miss votes for 'several weeks' as he rehabs

Connecticut Democratic Sen. Chris Murphy said Friday he had been released from a hospital after suffering a stroke last weekend.

Murphy, 53, said he will be in a rehabilitation center for the next "several weeks," meaning he will miss votes in the Senate when Congress returns on Monday.

"The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I've made so far," he said in a statement.

"In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today," said Murphy, who has been discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028.

Dr. Jose Suarez, chief of Neurosciences Critical Care at The Johns Hopkins Hospital, in a statement released by Murphy's office, said, "Senator Murphy experienced a small hemorrhagic stroke."

"He has shown tremendous progress, and at the current rate of improvement, we expect that he will make a full recovery following rehabilitation," Suarez said.

Murphy had been in the hospital since having a stroke at his son's hockey game.

"Last Saturday afternoon, I felt something was not right with my body and called 9-1-1 out of an abundance of caution, and I'm glad I did," Murphy said in his statement. "I was diagnosed with a small bleed in the thalamus region of my brain."

Murphy's stroke came three days before Democratic candidates, in a stunning election result, won more than enough races to flip the Senate and House of Representatives from Republican majority control in January.

Murphy, who was not up for reelection this year, before the election had campaigned for Democratic candidates around the country, most recently in Michigan, Minnesota and Florida. He had been scheduled to campaign in New Hampshire on Sunday with Rep. Chris Pappas, who on Tuesday won election to the Senate.

#2949. Fixtures and standings as Nations Championship returns

The Nations Championship resumed on Friday with Ireland securing a 42-12 win over Argentina, as the battle for continental supremacy heats up.

After three rounds of home games for southern hemisphere nations in July, the northern hemisphere teams are the hosts for the final three rounds.

Ireland's win over Argentina moved them top of the standings in the north, but France and Scotland can move above the Irish with victories on Saturday.

Six Nations champions France face Fiji on Saturday while Scotland - who are one point behind Les Bleus - hope to beat against New Zealand for the first time at Murrayfield on Saturday before games against Australia and Japan.

Third-placed England have 10 points and play Australia - who are third in the southern standings - at Allianz Stadium on Sunday before taking on Japan and New Zealand.

Ireland, who also have 10 points, face the challenge of taking on South Africa in the final round after playing Fiji in their second home game.

Wales beat Fiji before losing to Argentina and South Africa in their opening three matches. Their home games are against Japan, New Zealand and Australia.

Bottom-side Italy lost all three of their away games and face South Africa on Saturday.

The Springboks beat England, Scotland and Wales at home in their opening three Tests to lead New Zealand on points difference.

Player-coach rift report will only galvanise England - George

Scotland come full circle for another crack at All Blacks

Japan date starts pivotal month for Welsh rugby

Townsend never felt he could beat All Blacks as a player, but as a coach...

How are Ireland set for the Nations Championship?

How will Wales fare on Nations Championship return?

The Nations Championship pits the northern hemisphere teams who compete in the Six Nations - England, Ireland, Scotland, Wales, France and Italy - against southern hemisphere giants South Africa, New Zealand, Australia and Argentina, plus invited sides Fiji and Japan.

All teams will play each of the six in the opposing hemisphere once, with three rounds of fixtures staged in July and another three in November.

Their results will rank the teams within their own hemisphere, from one to six.

On the final weekend in November, there is a three-day play-off event staged at Allianz Stadium in Twickenham. The sixth-placed team in the northern hemisphere plays off against the equivalent in the southern hemisphere standings and so on, culminating in the two top-ranked sides taking each other on.

The winner of that final match is crowned the inaugural Nations Championship winner.

There is also a parallel, Ryder-Cup style hemisphere title. The winner of each of the matches on the play-offs weekend will earn one point for their hemisphere, except for the contest between the two top-ranked teams which delivers two.

The first hemisphere to earn four points over the weekend will be crowned winners.

The Nations Championship trophy and the shield awarded to the best hemisphere are pictured at the Tower of London in London [Getty Images]

Sixth-place North v Sixth-place South (16:40 GMT)

Third-place North v Third-place South (20:10 GMT)

Fifth-place North v Fifth-place South (13:10 GMT)

Second-place North v Second-place South (16:40 GMT)

Fourth-place North v Fourth-place South (13:10 GMT)

First-place North v First-place South (16:40 GMT)

#2946. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2945. Reports: Formula One has Tuesday deadline to switch race to Imola

A decision on a possible Formula One season finale at Italy's Imola track rather than in the Middle East must be made by Tuesday, motorsport media reported on Saturday.

The season is meant to conclude with races in Qatar on November 29 and Abu Dhabi a week later. But conflict and tensions in the Middle East mean F1 is mulling having just one race in Imola instead, although it has publicly backed Qatar and Abu Dhabi.

Organizers in the Italian region of Emilia-Romagna need to know imminently if they should step in as a replacement. After Tuesday, there would not be enough time to prepare.

Haas team boss Ayao Komatsu also confirmed Tuesday's deadline, according to broadcaster ESPN.

Imola as a plan B would be held in December. It would mean a home race for new world champion Kimi Antonelli of Mercedes.

If time runs out for Imola and an escalation makes the grands prix in Qatar and Abu Dhabi impossible for security reasons, the season could even end on November 21 in Las Vegas.

But according to ESPN, Formula One has promised teams at least 22 races this season. A Las Vegas finale would be race number 21.

#2944. A disturbing employment trend is hiding beneath the latest strong job numbers

Americans who lose their jobs these days are facing a harsh new problem: It's getting harder to find another one quickly.

The average jobless worker's bout of unemployment surpassed 27 weeks in October on a seasonally adjusted basis, according to Friday's jobs report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That amounts to a job search of nearly seven months, the longest outside of the pandemic period since 2016, when the U.S. labor market was finally getting back on track after the 2008 financial crisis.

About 2 million Americans have been without work for at least 27 weeks, the BLS reported. People in this category are referred to as the "long-term unemployed" and accounted for 27.6% of all jobless workers in October, the highest share since late 2021.

"This is a series that can be noisy from month to month," said Cory Stahle, senior economist at Indeed, a hiring platform. "But if you look over a longer time horizon, there is no mistake in the trend."

It's a troubling sign in an otherwise steady labor market. Workers stuck on the sidelines are a casualty of what's become a "low-hire, low-fire" job market, in which companies are hesitant to lay workers off, but aren't adding them either.

Research shows that Americans who experience long-term unemployment face depleted savings, lower earnings potential over time and declining mental health.

What's more, workers who are unemployed for extended periods risk falling into a self-perpetuating cycle, researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond wrote earlier this year.

"Time spent unemployed may itself reduce the probability of finding a job, perhaps because skills depreciate, networks thin or employers interpret long unemployment durations as a negative signal," the researchers warned.

Workers stop being able to claim most, if not all, government unemployment benefits after 26 weeks in a year. Given that, a rising share of long-term unemployed people can explain in part why initial and continuing jobless claims have remained low.

"Now, the average unemployed person takes longer to find a job than their benefits would last," Stahle said. "That is a really key point of this."

While jobless workers appear to be having a tough time, this week's employment data shows strength in other areas of the labor market.

Nonfarm payrolls blew past economists' forecasts for October and added 165,000 jobs, according to Friday's release. The unemployment rate ticked down to 4.1%, slightly lower than economists predicted.

Job openings came in much higher than economists expected in September, according to a separate report from the BLS released on Tuesday. The number of workers quitting jobs — a barometer of their confidence to obtain new employment — spiked to levels not seen since March 2025.

The BLS also said layoffs and discharges dropped to their lowest volume in more than a year. However, a recent string of high-profile workforce cuts could throw cold water on views among workers that the market is heating up.

CNBC reported this week that Disney is planning to axe thousands of jobs, the latest of several waves of layoffs at the media giant this year. American Express said last month that it was slashing head count by 5% as it reorients around artificial intelligence.

"It's difficult to separate what's happening to you or the people around you from the big picture," said Elizabeth Renter, lead economist at Life360. "When your LinkedIn is filled with layoffs and your social circle features a few folks who have been job seeking for several months, positive narratives about the labor market can seem jarring."

#2943. How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it

How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Nick Sirianni was asked point-blank on Monday if a quarterback change was on the table and he shot it down emphatically. The Eagles were sticking with Tanner McKee, Sirianni insisted.

"Tanner's our guy," Sirianni said on Monday afternoon. "We're not making any changes there.

The next day McKee got the call from Sirianni.

Despite what the head coach said to the world the day before, he was making a quarterback switch. McKee has been benched and Joe Milton, who arrived three weeks ago, will start in Week 9 against the Giants. The news broke on Wednesday.

"That's one thing I appreciate about (the team)," McKee said on Thursday. "They've been very upfront about things. I know what he said in the media on Monday, the day before. But he said he wanted to communicate to the guys before he says anything to the media. He doesn't want us finding out in the media, which I appreciate."

McKee, 26, said he isn't on social media, so he didn't know about Sirianni's declaration on Monday until after he found out he was demoted. So at least there's that.

Jalen Hurts began the year as the Eagles' starting quarterback but has been out since the Panthers game with blood clots that are claiming his season. McKee made the last three starts and the Eagles lost all of them. While McKee isn't the only reason they lost, he also wasn't able to do enough to win.

Over the last three weeks, McKee completed 63.2% of his passes for 638 yards with 2 touchdowns and 2 interceptions. He had a passer rating of 76.6 as the Eagles lost to the Panthers, Cowboys and Commanders. And there's no doubt that McKee struggled on Sunday in Washington, especially with his pocket awareness.

"I think, watching the film, different decisions, different throws," McKee said. "Obviously, as a quarterback, have to take accountability for things like that. I think you have to go back and review everything that was done even throughout the week. I thought I had a great process. Thought I felt prepared. I don't have any regrets there. I think you have to go back and be like, 'What could I have done there?' And take that approach as well."

The Eagles initially drafted McKee in the sixth round out of Stanford back in 2023 and groomed him to be their backup quarterback. That was the job he held all of last season coming off a Super Bowl parade. This offseason, though, the Eagles brought in veteran Andy Dalton to compete for that back job and Dalton won. But Dalton was demoted after throwing an interception against the Bears in Week 3 and was eventually released.

It's definitely fair to question how the Eagles have handled the quarterback position all year. McKee went from competing for the backup job to being the 3, then the 2, then the 1 and is now the 2 again.

And he's not resigned to staying in that spot.

"This is the NFL. Competition is always open," McKee said. "I'll keep our conversation private. I don't know how much [Sirianni has] been open to the media about our conversation. But there's always competition. This is the NFL. So you gotta go out, you've got to perform every week. I'm going to approach it as a competition and be ready in case my number is called. Whether that's an injury or my number is just called."

It's not like McKee got to play with a full deck over the last three weeks. He was without DeVonta Smith and Dontavyion Wicks for the last two of those losses and Saquon Barkley hasn't been healthy either. Not to mention, the offensive line hasn't played up to its standard all season.

But when McKee was asked on Thursday why he wasn't given the benefit of the doubt with all the injuries, he made no excuses.

"We have great players and we had great players on the field," McKee said. "The expectation is to go out and win and unfortunately, I fell short doing that. And I gotta be better and make better plays."

Because Milton arrived less than three weeks ago, McKee has been helping him as much as possible this week. Milton missed all of the install during training camp and has had to catch up quickly before his first NFL start on Sunday.

Even though Milton took his job, McKee has been more than willing to help him this week.

"In sports, you gotta have a short memory," McKee said. "I'm still a Philadelphia Eagle. I want the team to perform so anything I can do to help the team, whether that's helping Joe or Jalen or whoever it is, that's my job, that's my role and that's what I'm going to do."

As a 2023 draft pick, McKee is in the final year of his rookie contract with the Eagles. Based on the way this season has gone, there seems to be a pretty good chance he won't be back with the team in 2027.

But McKee isn't trying to rush that fate. McKee was asked on Thursday if he wants to still be here the rest of this season or if he'd prefer a clean break.

"I love it here," he said. "It's a great place. I have a lot of confidence in the coaches, in our scheme, Sean (Mannion). And so I'm happy to be here. I love being here. And I love to compete. Like we said, in the NFL, things are going to go up and down. It's going to be a roller coaster. But you have to love that as a competitor and you have to embrace that. That's my approach. That's what I'm going to do."

#2942. Alex Eala confirms what she plans to do in the Hong Kong final after beating Maria Sakkari

Alex Eala has booked her place into the final of the Hong Kong Open for the first time in her career.

Eala had not dropped a set prior to the semi-finals in Hong Kong, but she was forced to go the distance before beating former world number three Maria Sakkari 7-5, 3-6, 6-4 to book her spot in the final.

This is the second final Eala has reached in 2026, having won the Washington Open earlier this year, and she will now be hoping to add the Hong Kong title to her growing resume.

When speaking on court after beating Sakkari, Eala revealed her plans ahead of the Hong Kong Open final.

It was a battle for Eala all the way through the match, including in the deciding set where she was pegged back from 4-1 down to 4-4, before recomposing to win the final two games of the match and claim victory.

When asked about this in her on-court interview, Eala explained what her mindset was when Sakkari levelled the match up.

"I think in that moment she really stepped up and brought what she needed to bring to come back," said Eala. "I tried every point to stay there and step up myself."

Sakkari was not the only thing that Eala had to contend with, as there were also a number of interruptions in the match due to rain, but she was once again able to use her mentality to stop this derailing her chances.

"I think I was just blank," admitted Eala. "It was a lot of patience but a lot of focus gave me time to really focus in on the next point. I am really happy I managed to come out of that as a better player."

Eala will now look ahead to the final in Hong Kong, where she will face American Claire Liu, as she explained that she is going to try to be positive in the time she has before this championship match.

"I think the ability and conscious choice to be there and stay there and be positive was a really big point of my match today and my mentality," added Eala. "I guess not just Washington but everything I learned this year, to try and cap it off with a good match, I am going to try and be positive going into the final."

As well as remaining positive, Eala will also be hoping to repeat the numbers of having hit 79% first serves in against Sakkari, who she also created 22 break point opportunities against.

Eala is ranked over 80 places higher than her final opponent Liu, but it is actually the American who won their only previous meeting, which took place at the Guangzhou Open last year. Eala will be hoping to avoid this happening again when she faces Liu in the Hong Kong final on Sunday, November 8.

#2941. Drones are changing warfare. But defense giants are still making billions from old-school weapons

Drone technology and artificial intelligence are rapidly reshaping modern warfare, but the world's biggest defense contractors are finding that demand for tanks, fighter jets and other traditional military equipment remains strong.

Recent earnings from U.S. and European defense companies show conventional weapons continue to drive year-on-year growth, even alongside surging demand for advanced sensors, autonomous systems and counter-drone technology.

Rather than displacing traditional weapons manufacturers, the rise of drones and autonomous warfare is creating new business for many of the same companies.

From counter-drone defenses to advanced sensors integrated into tanks and fighter jets, the technologies transforming the battlefield are also changing, or even expanding, the markets served by legacy defense giants.

Lockheed Martin reported a 24% jump in revenue from its missiles business in the third quarter, while RTX secured multibillion-dollar contracts for established missile systems. In Europe, German defense supplier Renk saw orders for its military vehicle business surge 46% in the first nine months of the year.

Yet, the same earnings also show how the boundaries between legacy weapons and emerging defense technology are increasingly blurred.

German defense electronics company Hensoldt reported a 52% jump in revenue from its optronics business, with its chief financial officer telling CNBC that much of the growth came from conventional military equipment, including tanks and armored vehicles.

Meanwhile, Rheinmetall's air defense business, which includes counter-drone systems, reported 63% revenue growth, even as the wider company cut its full-year outlook, citing project delays.

The rise of drones is transforming traditional military platforms rather than replacing them, with tanks and other weapons increasingly integrated into connected, data-driven battlefield networks, according to Morningstar.

Over the past five years and since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in early 2022, defense primes have seen their order books swell amid higher military spending among governments globally.

Yet, analysts mostly say that the rearmament boom is structural, and even an end to wars in Ukraine and Iran won't derail it. Crucially, Europe now has to play catch-up amid increasing uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees and Russia's next move, after decades of military underfunding.

However, concerns that valuations had run ahead of the industry's ability to deliver dented defense stocks this year amid several instances of project delays and contract cancellations.

Advancements in modern warfare and technology – as proven by Ukraine's use of drones on the front lines – further hit investor confidence in defense contractors amid worries that new technologies may displace demand for traditional military equipment.

Beyond the front line, NATO plans to invest more than $40 billion in counter-drone capabilities over the next five years, as Secretary General Mark Rutte warned that drones have fundamentally transformed modern warfare.

Even so, Hensoldt CFO Christian Ladurner told CNBC on Thursday that demand for traditional military equipment remains a key driver of growth.

Its fast-growing optronics business, which supplies optical and sensor technologies, is benefiting from European governments' efforts to replenish conventional military equipment, even as newer battlefield technologies attract increasing attention, Ladurner said.

He also highlighted growing demand for air defense systems, as militaries confront an expanding range of threats, from small drones to ballistic missiles, adding that the air defense market is "underinvested" despite growing by 20% a year.

Limits to Patriot interceptors produced by Lockheed Martin are being felt hard, not least in Ukraine, after the U.S. depleted much of its stockpile of the state-of-the-art system able to take down high-speed missiles in the Iran war.

More recently, defense stocks have come under pressure amid concerns that the Democrats' U.S. midterm victories could squeeze American military spending.

While the result has dampened expectations for defense spending, with Democratic control of Congress making a proposed $1.5 trillion budget unlikely and potentially weighing on military spending growth through 2029, nothing has materially changed the geopolitical calculations, TD Cowen analyst Roman Schweizer told CNBC's "Morning Call" on Thursday.

"This is a multi-year picture, and I think we've seen those changes both in the U.S. and also Europe," he said, referencing internal budgetary difficulties and questions around NATO's hiked spending targets.

Morningstar analyst Loredana Muharremi told CNBC Wednesday's weakness in European defense stocks was largely sentiment-driven.

"Some investors may now see a greater chance of stronger US backing for Europe, easing the pressure on European governments to spend more [but] even with stronger US backing, we expect Europe to stick to its spending plans," she said.

"It takes years of investment to close the gaps in Europe's own military capabilities and build the industrial capacity it needs to deter Russia."

#2940. OpenAI bugging suspect Alfozan pleads not guilty

The Saudi national charged with planting a listening device in the headquarters of OpenAI while he worked there pleaded not guilty on Friday during an appearance in San Francisco federal court.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, was sent back to jail after the brief hearing. Alfozan has been held without bail since his arrest on a criminal complaint in early October.

He faces a maximum possible sentence of five years in prison if convicted in the case.

A grand jury indicted Alfozan on Oct. 27 on a single count of interception of oral communications, court records show.

Prosecutors say that he put a recording device in a television in an executive conference room at OpenAI's offices in San Francisco on Sept. 28.

Alfozan's lawyer declined to comment after the hearing before Magistrate Judge Peter Kang.

The judge ordered Alfozan to next appear in court on Nov. 17.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm, Raed Ventures.

"He currently serves as Engineering Lead at OpenAI, with a focus on AI Agents and the data platform," Alfozan's bio page on Raed's site says.

Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of Saudi Arabia's royal family, is a venture partner in the same firm.

Alfozan's case comes as U.S. companies such as OpenAI are making rapid advancements in developing artificial intelligence agents, and as concerns grow about the potential theft of AI tech from those companies.In June, President Donald Trump, in an executive order on AI innovation, said it was U.S. policy "to protect American ingenuity and intellectual property from exploitation and theft by adversaries; and to cultivate America's advanced AI-enabled capabilities."Trump has had a close relationship with Saudi Arabia. But he has also taken steps during his second term in the White House to limit the number of visas for highly skilled foreign workers.OpenAI earlier this year held discussions with Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the country's sovereign wealth fund, about investing in the company.

— Jordan Novet reported from San Francisco

#2939. In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips

In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Thank God for Makai Lemon and Eli Stowers. Because without those two rookies, I wouldn't have much of a "Positive Section" in this week's Roob's Eagles Stats.

Even with them there aren't a whole lot of encouraging numbers surrounding this football team.

So with that in mind, here's this week's Roob's Eagles Stats. When you lose six in a row, the stats aren't pretty.

1. With Makai Lemon catching six passes for 57 yards and Eli Stowers three for 53 yards, this was only the second time in the last 36 years the Eagles have had two rookies over 50 receiving yards in the same game. The last time it happened was in 2021, when DeVonta Smith had 122 yards and Kenny Gainwell 58 in a loss to the Chiefs at the Linc. Before that in 1990 Fred Barnett had 55 yards and Calvin Williams 72 in a win over the Patriots at the Vet. Two rookie replacement players did it in a 1987 strike replacement game against the Cowboys, including Haverford High's Mike Siano, but last time it happened in a real road game was in 1973 with Charlie Young (112) and Don Zimmerman (51) in a loss to the 49ers at Candlestick Park.

2. Lemon now has 31 catches, 3rd-most among NFL rookies and only two fewer than rookie leader K.C. Concepcion of the Browns, who has 33. Germie Bernard of the Steelers has 32. Lemon is on pace for 65 catches, which would be 3rd-most in Eagles history by a rookie, behind Keith Jackson (81 in 1988) and Jordan Matthews (67 in 2014). With seven catches against Dallas and six against Washington, Lemon tied the franchise record of consecutive games by a rookie with six or more receptions. Seven others have done it, most recently Jeremy Maclin in 2009 and DeVonta Smith in 2021.

3. With his fourth sack on Sunday night, Jalen Carter now has 11 ½ in his career and moved into a tie for 7th-most career sacks by an Eagles interior lineman. He's just 1 ½ behind Javon Hargrave for sixth. The top five are Andy Harmon and Fletcher Cox (20 ½), Jerome Brown (19), Darwin Walker (17) and Kenny Clarke (16 ½). With his fourth sack Nolan Smith surpassed his total of 3.0 from all of last year and is now just 2 ½ sacks shy of his career high of 6 ½ in 2024, not including four more from the postseason.

OK, thus begins the reality section. Brace yourself. This is bad. Really bad.

4. The Eagles have had fewer than 350 yards of offense in all eight games so far this year, with a high of 341 at Tennessee. This is the first time in more than half a century they've opened a season with eight games under 350 net yards. Last time it happened was the first 11 games of the 1971 season, when their yardage high was 315 yards in a 37-14 loss to the Bengals in the season opener at Riverfront Stadium. Going back to last year, the Eagles have gone 10 straight games with fewer than 350 yards, their longest streak since an 11-gamer over the last nine games of 1977 and the first games of 1978. This is not good.

5. The Eagles have been outscored by 65 points through eight games, the most points they've been outscored by in their first eight games since 1998, when they were outscored by 117 points. You have to go back to 1972 to find the last time before 1998 they were outscored by this many points eight games into a season. The only NFL team outscored by a larger margin this year is the 0-8 Chargers, who have been outscored by 76 points. The Eagles have been outscored by 11 points by the 3-4 Panthers and by 17 points by the 2-4-1 Commanders and 3-5 Cowboys.

6. This is the first time since 1970 the Eagles have lost three games in a season by 11 or more points to teams with losing records. It's only the third season in franchise history they've lost two games by 17 or more points to teams with losing records. It also happened in 1972 and 2015. The only other time they lost back-to-back games by at least 17 points to losing teams came in 2015, when they lost 45-17 to the Bucs, who finished 6-10, and 45-14 to a Lions team that finished 7-9. Chip Kelly was fired four weeks later.

7. The Eagles led 10-0 Sunday before losing 27-10. Last time the Eagles led a game by 10 points in the first quarter and lost by at least 17 points was in 2011, when they took a 10-0 lead on the Patriots at the Linc on a LeSean McCoy touchdown and Alex Henery field goal before getting outscored 38-10 the rest of the game and losing 38-20, Tom Brady over Vince Young. This was the first game in franchise history where the Eagles led by 10 points in the first quarter and didn't score again.

8. With a 25-14 loss to the Panthers, 38-21 to the Cowboys and 27-10 to the Commanders, this is only the third time in the last 50 years the Eagles have lost three straight games by 11 or more points, allowing at least 25 points in all three. It also happened in 2012 and 2016. Last time the Eagles were outscored by 71 points in a six-game span was 2012.

9. Rasul Douglas's interception near the end of the game was the 23rd of his 10-year career with the Eagles, Panthers, Packers, Cards, Bills, Dolphins and Commanders. Douglas is one of only three Eagles drafted in the last 30 years with at least 23 career interceptions. The others are Sheldon Brown (26) and Jordan Poyer (25). Douglas, the Eagles' 3rd-round pick in 2017, is the first cornerback drafted by the Eagles to record an interception against the Eagles since Mark McMillian of the Chiefs picked off Rodney Peete in a 24-21 Chiefs win at the Vet in 1998.

10. The Eagles netted just 268 yards Sunday night, their fifth game during the six-game losing streak with fewer than 270 yards. They're averaging 244 yards per game during these last six games, their worst six-game span in 27 years, since they averaged 241 during a six-game span in 1999, Andy Reid's first season as head coach. This is the first time they've had five games under 270 yards in a six-game span since early in the 2003 season. No other NFL team has had five games under 270 yards this year. Their overall average of 265 yards per game is their worst through eight games since 1999, when they were averaging 254 through their eighth game.

#2938. Liverpool star breaks silence on potential new contract

Liverpool are moving towards a new agreement with Trey Nyoni, a development that feels significant without carrying any immediate pressure. As Liverpool Echo reported, discussions with the midfielder's representatives have been ongoing for several months and there is increasing confidence that a deal will be reached.

That matters because Nyoni's place in the first-team picture has changed considerably. When Liverpool sanctioned Curtis Jones' sale to Inter Milan in August, there was a conscious decision not to bring in a replacement. The judgement internally was that Nyoni was ready for a more senior role, and the early weeks of the season have strengthened that view.

There was also a firm show of intent from the club in the market. A £30m bid from Nottingham Forest late in the window was turned away, and Liverpool have since watched the 19-year-old become an increasingly trusted option for Andoni Iraola. His full Premier League debut in the 1-0 defeat to Arsenal offered further encouragement, with Nyoni acquitting himself well before making way for Dominik Szoboszlai.

The numbers behind his progress are compelling. Nyoni has already surpassed last season's Premier League appearance tally and now sits on 12 senior outings in all competitions, including his first Liverpool goal in the 3-2 Champions League defeat to Fenerbahce. Those are visible signs of development, but the underlying data is arguably even more persuasive.

He leads Liverpool's squad for Opta-defined defensive contributions, averaging 13.3 per 90 minutes. He also tops Liverpool midfielders for line-breaking passes with 18.2, a useful marker of his ability to move the game forward. In Europe, where he has started three of four group matches, his 92 per cent pass completion under high-intensity pressure is the best recorded in the competition's new format so far.

There is no contractual rush, with more than three years still left on his current terms after signing fresh papers in June 2025. This is more about recognition than necessity. Liverpool want Nyoni's status to match his growing importance, and that is often how elite clubs protect value while rewarding progress.

Nyoni's own stance suggests calm on all sides. He told Liverpool Echo: "At the minute I am just leaving to my agents to sort out. My main focus right now is playing football, performing every time I go on the pitch. The off the pitch stuff, I leave it to them to do."

That response will reassure Liverpool. There is no noise, no public pressure, and no hint of distraction. Instead, there is a young midfielder focused on opportunity and a club acting early to secure a player they clearly believe can shape the next phase of this squad.

This is exactly the sort of update supporters will want to hear. Liverpool have spotted a young player developing quickly, given him a pathway, rejected a sizeable offer, and are now ready to reward him before the wider market can properly circle. That feels smart, proactive and entirely in keeping with how top clubs should handle emerging talent.

What stands out most is that Nyoni is earning this moment through substance. The statistics are outstanding, but they also match what has been seen on the pitch. He looks composed, technically sharp and willing to take responsibility in difficult areas. For a 19-year-old to post those numbers for defensive work and progressive passing says a lot about his all-round midfield profile.

There is also something encouraging in the timing. Iraola clearly trusts him, and Liverpool's decision not to replace Curtis Jones looks far more understandable when viewed through Nyoni's rise. It shows conviction from the coaching staff and recruitment side.

If this contract is finalised soon, it will feel like another important step in securing the spine of the future. Liverpool need young players who can contribute now and grow into major roles later. Nyoni looks capable of doing both, and that is why this news lands so positively.

#2937. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2936. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#2935. Eagles-Giants matchups to watch in Week 9

Eagles-Giants matchups to watch in Week 9 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles (2-6) host the Giants (4-2-1) on Sunday afternoon at Lincoln Financial Field.

While the Eagles are on a six-game losing streak, the Giants are in first place in the NFC East and coming off a bye week.

The Eagles have been having a tough time with blitzes all year and they're probably going to see a lot of them this week against the Giants and defensive coordinator Dennard Wilson. Maybe Joe Milton will be able to avoid them better than Tanner McKee.

Milton, 26, will make his first-career start on Sunday. While he isn't exactly a dual-threat QB, Milton has more ability to avoid pressure.

This season, the Eagles have faced 109 blitzes this season and have given up 66 pressures — a pressure rate of 60.6%, which is the highest in the NFL. They have also allowed a free rusher more than any other team in the league. So it would be crazy for teams to not blitz them at this point. And the Giants already blitz at the second highest rate in the NFL, per NextGen Stats. And the Vikings barely count because Brian Flores is playing a different sport right now:

1. Vikings: 71.0%2. Giants: 40.3%3. Buccaneers: 40.2%4. Patriots: 40.1%5. Panthers: 39.6%

Wilson was with the Eagles in 2021 and 2022 and was passed over for the defensive coordinator job in 2023, when the Eagles went with Sean Desai. He has since served as DC with the Titans and now Giants.

We've watched Saquon Barkley (hamstring/back) try to gut it out for the last two games and with the bye week coming up, he'll probably try to do it again against his former team on Sunday afternoon. Barkley managed to play 22 snaps against the Commanders in Week 8 and wasn't very productive. He had 13 carries for just 30 yards and a touchdown, as well as a 24-yard catch. But Barkley is still obviously the Eagles' best option. Even playing through these injuries, Barkley has shown how good he still is. And the Eagles have already told him they're not moving him ahead of the trade deadline.

The Giants this season have the No. 26-ranked run defense, giving up 128.1 yards per game. Before their bye week, the Giants held the Texans to just 84 rushing yards in an overtime game but gave up 175 to the Saints and 202 to the Commanders. So they might still be susceptible on the ground. The Giants are giving up 1.66 yards before contact per attempt, which ranks 27th in the league. The Eagles will have the same OL again this week for the third straight game.

The Giants have some very good edge rushers but the team hasn't gotten many sacks this season. They are tied with the Chiefs and Dolphins at the bottom of the league with 9.0 in seven games. (The Eagles have just 10 in eight games.)

But the Giants still have Abdul Carter, Kayvon Thibodeaux and rookie Arvell Reese to bring the pressure. And then they have a DC who is always willing to add more pressure by blitzing. This won't be an easy game for Jordan Mailata and Markel Bell. Bell, the rookie from Miami, had a rough game against the Commanders in Week 8 and has had some ups and downs since taking over for Lane Johnson. But the Eagles need to really figure out what they have in him over the final nine games of the season. This will be a good test on Sunday.

The Giants are coming off the bye week but it's unclear if that time off was enough to get Cam Skattebo back from a hamstring injury. The Giants' starting running back hurt his hammy in a run late in the Giants' overtime win over the Texans before the bye. If Skattebo is unavailable, the Giants would have Devin Singletary and Gregory Desroisiers as their top backs.

Skattebo this season leads the Giants in rushing with 418 yards on 98 carries (4.3) and 2 touchdowns. Skattebo is a battering ram, who is averaging 3.6 yards after contact per rush. The Eagles have had some tackling issues this season, so they'll need to get him to the ground.

Much has been made about the Eagles' inability to rush the passer this year, and that's true. But it starts because they're unable to stop the run. Jordan Davis is supposed to be their run-stuffing anchor but he has been dealing with a hand injury that has slowed him down. The Eagles are 28th in run defense this season, allowing 131.9 yards per game. The Eagles have given up 100+ rushing yards in every single game this season. That's their longest such streak to start a season since 1968.

The Giants used the No. 6 overall pick to draft Nabers out of LSU in the 2024 season and he's on pace to have a career year in 2026. As a rookie, Nabers went for over 1,200 yards but tore his ACL in Year 2 and missed most of the season. Nabers has come back from that ACL tear better than ever this season. Through seven games, he has 48 catches on 70 targets for 605 yards and 2 touchdowns. He's averaging 86.4 yards per game and has had over 100 yards in each of his last four games. Nabers lines up a bit in the slot but is mostly outside. The Eagles haven't used Quinyon Mitchell to travel with top receivers yet this season but it might be a good week to try it.

Mitchell has been excellent this season but proved he's human when he gave up a 53-yard pass to Treylon Burks in the third quarter. But Mitchell has been excellent all season. He has given up 27 catches on 50 targets for 293 yards. So 18% of the yards he has given up this season have come on one play. He has an opponent passer rating of 69.8 this year.

Through eight games this season, Jalen Carter already has four sacks and is on pace to have a career-high in 2026. But that obviously doesn't tell the whole story. At times, Carter looks dominant, but he hasn't been consistent enough. Even Vic Fangio has admitted that this season. But if the Eagles' defense is going to figure things out in 2026, then Carter has to be the motor behind it.

In this game, Carter will get some matchups against Giants first-round pick Francis Mauigoa. The Giants used the No. 10 overall pick on the offensive lineman out of Miami. But because they didn't have a spot at offensive tackle, the Giants have moved him inside the right guard for his rookie season. While Mauigoa has had some ups and downs this season, he has flashed some real potential.

#2933. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2930. Derrick Henry trade rumors end as Ravens make decision before NFL deadline

The Baltimore Ravens won't trade Derrick Henry before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline, keeping their future Hall of Fame running back through a four-game losing streak and a quarterback room that lost its top two players to injuries.

Baltimore never considered moving the five-time Pro Bowler and recently told Henry it has no plans to deal him, according to league sources. The New York Giants did ask about a Ravens running back, but it was Justice Hill.

MORE: Week 9 buy-low trade targets: Mahomes, Mason, Cooper, LaPorta at fantasy discounts

Trade talk grew after quarterback Lamar Jackson needed surgery on his right thumb and backup Tyler Huntley's turf toe ended his season. Henry's history with two current head coaches also drew attention to possible landing spots. John Harbaugh coached him the past two seasons in Baltimore before taking the Giants job, and Mike Vrabel coached him in Tennessee before landing with the New England Patriots.

Moving the 32-year-old still would've left Baltimore with roughly $12 million in dead money. His contract runs through 2027, when he's due a non-guaranteed $11 million base salary, so the Ravens can revisit his future in the offseason.

Henry made clear after Thursday's loss that he isn't pushing for an exit.

"I have not mentioned me going anywhere else," Henry said. "I'm saying, 'Let's go back to work. Let's try to get better and turn this thing around.'"

Arizona Cardinals quarterback Jacoby Brissett (7) takes the field before they play the Denver Broncos | USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

Baltimore made its deadline move Friday, when the Jacoby Brissett trade became official. The Ravens received the veteran quarterback and a 2027 fifth-round pick from the Arizona Cardinals for a 2027 fourth-rounder and a 2028 seventh-rounder.

Brissett started Arizona's first eight games, throwing for 1,719 yards with 10 touchdowns and seven interceptions. He also spent the past two seasons in Arizona with Ravens quarterbacks coach Israel Woolfork, which should speed up his learning curve.

Henry needs a passer that defenses have to respect. Undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano went 10-of-18 for 60 yards and two interceptions in Thursday's 33-10 loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars, so Jacksonville crowded the line and held Henry to 57 yards on 17 carries, even with him taking direct snaps in the Wildcat. He's now been held under 60 rushing yards in four straight games.

"It wasn't good enough. We've got to be better," Henry said. "[It was the] same story."

The loss dropped the Ravens to 4-5 and marked their third straight defeat by at least 20 points, a franchise-record first. First-year coach Jesse Minter's offense has also scored fewer than 14 points in four consecutive games for the first time in team history.

Baltimore Ravens running back Derrick Henry (22). Credit: Kirby Lee-Imagn Images.

The former Heisman Trophy winner passed Jerome Bettis and LaDainian Tomlinson on Thursday to move into seventh on the NFL's career rushing list with 13,707 yards. He's 394 yards behind Curtis Martin for sixth and has 689 rushing yards and eight touchdowns through nine games this season.

The Ravens sit last in the AFC North while the Pittsburgh Steelers lead at 6-2. Baltimore faces the last-place Los Angeles Chargers on Monday Night Football, then plays road games against the Carolina Panthers and Houston Texans before a bye and a home game against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

The Ravens hope Jackson can return later this season, and until then their offense runs through Brissett and Henry.

#2929. Giants vs Eagles betting guide: Odds, props & picks

Giants vs Eagles betting guide: Odds, props & picks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Eagles (2-5) vs Commanders (1-4-1), Sunday 8:20pm

Point spread/ML: Giants -3/-174 Eagles +3/+146

Analysis: Tale as old as time: first place team on its third quarterback, taking on a last-place team on its fourth QB. My only question is how in the world the Giants are only favored by three points against a team that hasn't won since the Phillies were still a week away from finishing their regular season.

The offense is ravaged by injury, and the defense is deteriorating before our eyes: 90 points and nearly 1,200 yards allowed in the last three games. Gimme Big Blue -3, and even make another bet at -6.5.

JJ McCarthy: 214.5 passing yards, 1.5 TD (+174/-240), 0.5 INT (-130/-102), 17.5 completions, 29.5 attempts

Joe Milton: 188.5 passing yards, 0.5 TD (-225/+164) 0.5 INT (-162/+122), 17.5 completions, 31.5 attempts

Malik Nabers – 72.5 yards, 5.5 receptions

Dallas Goedert – 35.5 yards, 3.5 receptions
