#2928. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2925. The race is on to build the first fusion power plant — and Europe is looking to Japan for help

The race is on to build the world's first commercial fusion power plant — and one of Europe's leading contenders in the field has just received a boost.

Munich-based Proxima Fusion signed memoranda of understanding with two Japanese industrial partners, Fujikura and Kyoto Fusioneering, in Tokyo on Oct. 28. Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz — who was visiting in a bid to deepen trade ties — were both in attendance.

Ann Mettler, president of Catalyse Europe, a Brussels-based clean-energy think tank, and a former vice president of Breakthrough Energy, said developing the technology should be a strategic imperative for Europe.

"Europe is a resource-poor geography and we are very dependent on China. The world is in competition for technological supremacy — this is a zero-sum game," Mettler told CNBC, speaking in a personal capacity.

"Fusion is a silver bullet because the promise is abundant, clean, limitless energy."

Fusion energy seeks to replicate the way the sun produces power, using super-heated plasma to fuse two light atomic nuclei into one heavier one, releasing vast amounts of energy in the process.

There are two main approaches: one uses high-powered lasers, while the other uses powerful magnets to confine the plasma. Proxima is developing a type of magnetic device called a stellarator, which is designed to hold the plasma in place for long periods.

The agreement with Kyoto Fusioneering will expand existing work on next-generation gyrotrons — devices that generate high-power microwaves used to heat the plasma.

Proxima's agreement with 141-year-old Fujikura will explore plans for industrial-scale manufacturing in Germany of high-temperature superconducting, or HTS, tape, a specialized metallic tape that is superconducting at relatively high temperatures and can carry electrical current with virtually no resistive losses.

The tape is wound into coils to form powerful magnets that confine and control the heated plasma inside the stellarator.

Francesco Sciortino, CEO and co-founder of Proxima Fusion, told CNBC that Fujikura, which employs over 50,000 people worldwide, was a leader in manufacturing high-temperature superconductors, having built its reputation in optical fibers. "They think this tape has the potential to be the next optical fibre," he said.

He said Proxima, which is studying plans to build a manufacturing facility in Lower Saxony, hoped to make around 5,000 kilometers, or 3,100 miles, of tape annually by 2030.

The 140 million euro ($160 million) facility would be financed partly by the proceeds of a $468 million funding round in July, which valued Proxima, whose backers include Alphabet-owned Google, at $2.7 billion.

Proxima expects to need around 20,000 kilometers (12,400 miles) of the tape for its "Alpha" fusion demonstrator and a further 40,000 kilometers (24,900 miles) for its planned "Stellaris" fusion power station.

Sciortino said that establishing production of the tape in Germany was crucial as it would help Europe create the expertise and supply chains needed to turn its fusion research leadership into industrial capability.

This is where Europe has historically struggled, according to Catalyse Europe's Mettler.

"Every geography wants to turn leadership in science into industrial leadership and this is where Europe has traditionally been not so good — turning that scientific leadership into euros," she said.

While Europe had industrial giants to be proud of, disruptors tended to be more successful in frontier technologies, according to Mettler.

"If Europe is first to develop this technology, it will give us a huge advantage, but key to that will be building the supply chain," she said.

Sciortino said he remains confident Proxima will be the first company to turn on a pilot plant — but stressed the key to profitability would be making that plant as simple as possible to operate.

"You can have complexity in the design, manufacturing and operation of the plant," he said. "We can take care of the complexity in the design and manufacturing, but our view is that there needs to be simplicity in the operation. We are aiming for net energy gain during the early 2030s."

But Proxima has some well-heeled competitors. They include U.S.-based Commonwealth Fusion Systems, which is also backed by Google, and Helion Energy, as well as Canada's General Fusion. China has a number of state-backed players, most notably China Fusion Energy Co., while Tokyo-based Helical Fusion leads Japan's push.

"The supply chain in fusion energy needs to be built years before — people often think of frontier technologies as a switch that can be thrown," Mettler said. "That is not the case here."

#2924. Future Eagles Fits: Four position versatile OTs to know in the 2027 NFL Draft

Future Eagles Fits: Four position versatile OTs to know in the 2027 NFL Draft originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Welcome back to Future Eagles Fits, our weekly deep dive into the prospects who could eventually find their way to Philadelphia.

Every week, we're going inside the tape and looking at prospects through an Eagles-centric lens — breaking down the athletic profile, positional versatility and scheme fit that could ultimately determine whether a player makes sense in Philly. Along the way, we'll examine how prospects are being evaluated across the league as draft boards begin to take shape and conversations are had with representatives across the scouting landscape.

If there's one position group that could be a priority for the Eagles heading into next spring, it's the offensive line. The Eagles have always placed an emphasis on building through the trenches, but the group has undergone significant changes over the last year. Cam Jurgens is a Baltimore Raven, Lane Johnson announced his retirement and rookie Markell Bell has been thrust into an opportunity at right tackle. Meanwhile, Jordan Mailata, one of the best left tackles in football, has regressed, Drew Kendall has had his share of ups and downs in a new role at the pivot spot and Tyler Steen hasn't been close to the level of play he displayed last fall.

There are plenty of questions surrounding the group, and whether the issues stem from coaching, the overall offensive scheme, individual performance or some combination of all three, the Eagles have work to do up front. Howie Roseman has never been shy about investing in the position group, and with multiple potential starting positions to address, adding more young talent could be a significant priority next spring.

The bottom line when it comes to offensive linemen, a position group that has consistently been a foundation of Philly's success, is that you can never have enough quality bodies up front. Whether it's finding a versatile interior blocker who can compete at multiple positions or adding another long-term option at tackle, the Eagles should be looking at opportunities to add talent throughout the draft.

Here are four positional versatile offensive tackles to know for the 2027 NFL Draft — one potential Day 1 target, two Day 2 prospects and a Day 3 option on the rise for teams.

Day 1: Lance Heard, Kentucky | 6'5″, 323 lbs, 36 ¼ arm (verified measurements)

Heard is one of the more physically imposing offensive line prospects in this class, and he's currently a top five player on my personal board.

A Tennessee transfer, Heard brings an impressive combination of size, length, raw power and violence that immediately stands out when watching his tape similar to guys like Tyler Smith out of Tulsa and Paris Johnson Jr. out of Ohio State a few years back. He looks to staple players to the turf on every single rep, and at 320-plus pounds with ridiculous 36-inch arms, he has the type of physical profile NFL teams drool over along the offensive line. Describing him as powerful would be an understatement, as he can flat out overwhelm defenders at the point of attack, generate movement in the run game and use his length to create separation between himself and opposing rushers with ease.

What makes Heard such an intriguing player is that there's still plenty of room for development. He has the natural strength and physical tools, but his consistency, technique and ability to maintain proper positioning throughout reps will be important areas of development. There are moments when his physical advantages take over, and others where he can become a little too aggressive with his approach. Working on becoming more consistent with his hands, balance and overall technique will be important as he makes the transition to the next level.

An interesting conversation surrounding Heard is where he ultimately projects at the NFL level. While he has experience playing tackle and possesses the length to remain outside, there are teams who believe his best position as a pro could actually be inside at guard. His power and ability to create movement inside make him an intriguing option along the interior, particularly for a team like the Eagles who value athleticism and physicality in the run game.

For the Eagles, that positional flexibility is particularly appealing. With questions surrounding Landon Dickerson's long-term future at left guard and the possibility of additional movement along the offensive line, Heard could provide an opportunity to address multiple needs with one selection. He has the physical profile to compete at tackle, but the possibility of moving inside could give the Eagles another option as it continues to reshape the group.

Day 2: Trevor Lauck, Iowa | 6'5″, 314 lbs, 33 ¼ arm (verified measurements)

Iowa continues to produce NFL-caliber offensive linemen, and Lauck is the next name to know from Kirk Ferentz's program.

A technically sound athlete up front with experience at left tackle, Lauck offers a different profile than some of the more physically imposing offensive tackles in this class. He's not necessarily going to overwhelm defenders with overwhelming length or elite raw power, but his ability to stay balanced, maintain proper positioning and execute his assignments makes him an appealing prospect. Linemen at Iowa come out extremely well coached, something that can't be said for a majority of prospects at the collegiate level no matter how talented a prospect they are.

On tape, Lauck's technique and overall consistency are what stand out. He's comfortable operating in space, displays good footwork in pass pro and does a nice job staying connected to defenders through the duration of a rep. His hand placement and ability to maintain leverage allow him to neutralize rushers without needing to rely exclusively on his physical tools, and in the run game, he's a coordinated blocker who understands angles to showcase the ability to work up to the second level and sustain blocks.

For the Eagles, Lauck is a name to keep an eye on because of the potential to add another technically sound young blocker to the unit. Philly has always valued players who can operate within a physical run scheme while providing consistency in pass protection, and Lauck checks and bolds several of those boxes. Whether he ultimately sticks at tackle or projects better inside, his ability to execute assignments and provide positional flexibility could make him an appealing Day 2 target.

Day 2: Trevor Goosby, Texas | 6'7″, 325 lbs, 34 ⅞ arm (verified measurements)

Goosby entered the 2026 season with legitimate first-round buzz and was widely viewed as one of the top offensive tackle prospects in the country. However, a torn patellar tendon in his left knee ended his campaign abruptly.

At 6-foot-7 with nearly 35-inch arms, Goosby has the type of frame and athletic profile that immediately draws the eyes of NFL scouts. And that's before you turn on the tape. He's a long, athletic tackle with the ability to cover ground in pass protection, mirror opposing edge rushers and use his length and outstanding patience to keep defenders away from the quarterback. His movement skills are particularly impressive for someone with his size, and he has shown the ability to recover when defenders attempt to counter his initial pass set.

Goosby's development as a pass protector has been encouraging, particularly after earning first-team All-SEC honors in 2025. He has the athleticism to handle speed off the edge, and his length gives him a significant advantage when he's able to establish his hands early in a rep. He's got vines for arms, and when he's able to 'win first' with his hands he can take the air out of a pass rush quickly.

The biggest question surrounding Goosby now is his health and what his next step looks like. A severe knee injury introduces an entirely different element into his draft evaluation, particularly with the possibility of returning to Texas for another season still on the table. Prior to the injury, he was viewed as a potential top-10 guy. If he elects to enter the draft, his medical evaluations and recovery timeline will play a significant role in determining where he ultimately comes off the board.

With Johnson's retirement and Bell still developing, the Eagles have a need for additional talent at the position. The injury creates some risk, but the upside is significant if he's able to make a full recovery. Depending on how the medical evaluations unfold and whether he declares, Goosby could become one of the more interesting value opportunities in the class.

Day 3: Samson Okunlola, Miami | 6'6″, 324 lbs, 33 ¾ arm (verified measurements)

Evaluating Okunlola is an exercise in patience, but his raw traits are undeniable. A former five-star recruit, he took time in Coral Gables before breaking into the rotation in 2025, serving as a rotational force at left guard throughout Miami's 16-game CFP run. The film he put together during that stretch was extremely promising as he allowed just five total pressures all year, per PFF, posting shutouts against Ohio State and Indiana (National Championship) when it mattered most. His foundation is built on elite athleticism, flexibility in his lower half and a massive frame that naturally creates movement in the run game by establishing angles and washing defenders down the line of scrimmage. His ability to sink and drive in his lower half is unique for a player of his stature, as larger athletes usually bend at the hips, not the knees, which can cause them to lose reps consistently against more agile defenders. Not Okunlola.

However, the developmental hurdles are real and also why context is important when discussing players and scheme fit. It's not a linear path from college dominance to NFL success, and things can take time. Physical fronts like Texas A&M managed to expose his frame when attacking him head-on, while speed and counters from Virginia Tech and Florida highlighted pass sets that remain uneven rep-to-rep. Originally brought to college as a tackle, his future could lie within the interior where his instincts as a run blocker can shine without leaving him on an island against NFL edge-rushers.

For the Eagles, Okunlola is an extremely intriguing fit for a motion-heavy, wide-zone offense. Mannion's scheme requires interior linemen who carry weight comfortably without compromising lateral quickness allowing them to stretch perimeter run lanes and reach second-level linebackers, and Okunlola's movement and ability to work into angles could fit naturally within Philadelphia's front.

#2923. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#2920. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#2919. How new Eagles QB Joe Milton is preparing for unlikely first career start

How new Eagles QB Joe Milton is preparing for unlikely first career start originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

A few weeks ago, Joe Milton was in meetings and film study with Dak Prescott and former Eagle Sam Howell in the Cowboys' quarterback room at the team facility in Frisco, Texas.

Now he's the Eagles' starting quarterback.

"You never know what can happen in the NFL," Milton said after practice Thursday. "Yes, I was on the practice squad and I was just preparing for any moment that presents itself. Still, when I was at Dallas on the practice squad, I was just pretty much going through the plays, going through the week like I was a starter and just being ready.

"And that's what the NFL is about. Just being ready when your name is called and just preparing for those moments."

The Eagles signed Milton off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16, and on Sunday – 23 days later and after just 10 practices – he'll make his first NFL start against the Giants at the Linc.

"My coaches here, Parks (Frazier) and Sean (Mannion), did a great job of getting me caught up early," Milton said. "And I spent a lot of time inside the building just trying to learn this offense and the communication aspect of it.

"It's a lot of new terminology for me, so being in my third offense, just trying to plug and play and go from there. … Just executing whatever the play call is that comes through my helmet. Just call that play and execute. Know my job. Know my 1/11th. Do my 1/11th, and make a play."

Milton spent 2024 with the Patriots playing in Alex Van Pelt's offense then joined the Cowboys last year playing under Klayton Adams.

A lot of learning, a lot of new schemes, new coaches, new teammates. Just constant change for the 26-year-old Milton.

"It's just a whole new offense," Milton said. "I just came from my previous team, and a lot of stuff was just different. So coming here was just pretty much learning a whole new system of the West Coast and just going with that.

"Kind of go back to my rookie year, rookie year offense."

The Eagles were 2-3 this year under Jalen Hurts, now out for the season with blood clots, and 0-3 under Tanner McKee, benched this week for Milton.

This will be the first season since 2002 where the Eagles will start three different quarterbacks in meaningful games. In 2002, Donovan McNabb started the first 10 games, before breaking his right leg vs. the Cards at the Linc, Koy Detmer started vs. the 49ers at Candlestick but suffered a dislocated elbow and A.J. Feeley started the next five games before McNabb returned for the final two regular-season games and the playoffs.

Asked what people who haven't seen him play – which is pretty much everybody – should expect, Milton didn't talk about his mobility or his rocket-cannon arm, he just spoke about playing disciplined football within the scheme.

"Doing what the coach asks, going out there and whatever play is called, just going out there and executing that play, and nothing else matters in the world except for that play," he said. "So whatever it takes, whatever the team needs me to do, that's what I'm willing to do. If I have to run, if I have to throw or hand it off, it's perfect."

Milton hasn't started a game although he played most of a win against the Bills for the Patriots on the final day of the 2024 season, throwing for one touchdown and running for another. He got into four games with the Cowboys last year – all in mop-up duty late in blowout losses – and threw a touchdown and two INTs.

In parts of five games with the Patriots and Cowboys, he's completed 37 of 53 passes (70 percent) for 424 yards with two TDs and two INTs with a 90.4 passer rating.

Milton is the first quarterback who'll start for the Eagles after not being here on opening day since Ken O'Brien in 1993. He's the first former Cowboy to start at quarterback for the Eagles since Rodney Peete in 1995. He's the third NFL QB to make his first career start this year, following Jalon Daniels of the Buccaneers and Joe Fagnano of the Ravens.

"I approach the week the same," he said. "Came in here, worked hard and stayed late, got the plays like I always do for every week since I've been here, and it's been going good so far."

Milton said his 1-year-old daughter, Maleia, helps keep him grounded when things are kind of nutty.

"I mean, I always (want to) be where my feet are," he said. "My daughter does a great job of reminding me of that. Yes, she can't talk yet, but at the same time, when I see her face, it just allows me to be thankful. So when I see her every night on FaceTime, I'm just thankful. It allows me to take a deep breath and remember where I'm at. And being where my feet are is probably the best thing because you think too far and you never know what (can) happen."

#2917. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2916. Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak

Nick Sirianni emphatically backs OL coach Chris Kuper amid losing streak originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Chris Kuper was dealt a tough hand, but this is bad.

When the Eagles hired Kuper as their offensive line coach this offseason to replace the legendary Jeff Stoutland, the hope was that the Eagles would be healthy up front and return to dominance. That hasn't gone to plan.

And through eight games, the Eagles' struggling offensive line is one of the big reasons they are 2-6 and losers of six straight. But head coach Nick Sirianni still believes in Kuper. He gave an impassioned defense of his OL coach on Monday afternoon.

"Chris is coaching his [butt] off," Sirianni said on Monday. "He's fighting his [butt] off, he's coaching his [butt] off on a daily basis. You guys don't get to see that. I get it on a daily basis.

"You get to see that for three hours on Sunday, but he's coaching his [butt] off and he's fighting his [butt] off and I know he's giving the players good tools to use fundamentally and helping them be the best players that they possibly can be. But we all have to be better and we know that."

The next morning on 94WIP, Sirianni said more of the same about Kuper when asked about the way Stoutland departed the organization. He also said the Eagles' players have faith in Kuper and you can ask them. That might be true, but that can't matter more than results; and the results aren't good.

Through eight games, the Eagles are 30th in points and 32nd in total yards.

It's impossible to say if having Stoutland this season would have helped the Eagles greatly. But it's also impossible not to wonder, especially because his departure came after his role was diminished by Sirianni during the 2025 season. Stoutland is one of the greatest coaches in franchise history but even his line struggled last year.

It's also fair to point out that the line the Eagles have been using for much of the season is very different than what they expected entering the year. At the start of the season, the Eagles were banking on Lane Johnson, Landon Dickerson and Cam Jurgens. Well, Johnson retired after playing in three games, Jurgens was traded to the Ravens and Dickerson has played just 3 of 8 games.

Here's a look at the Eagles' OL throughout this season:

Week 1: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Cam Jurgens, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 2: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 3: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Lane JohnsonWeek 4: Jordan Mailata, Cam Jurgens, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 5: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 6: Jordan Mailata, Willie Lampkin, Jake Majors, Tyler Steen, Markel BellWeek 7: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel BellWeek 8: Jordan Mailata, Landon Dickerson, Drew Kendall, Tyler Steen Markel Bell

In addition to the constant state of flux on the line, the Eagles have dealt with a ton of injuries to other players, including Jalen Hurts, DeVonta Smith, Saquon Barkley and more.

So it wasn't reasonable to expect the Eagles' offensive line to completely save them. But it's not unreasonable to expect better execution when it comes to things like assignments and communication. The Eagles have struggled with simple things up front at times this year. The Eagles have the 27th-ranked rushing offense in the NFL in 2026 and have given up a pressure rate of 42.65, which ranks 29th.

And the Eagles have been absolutely flummoxed by blitzes all year. They entered Week 8 allowing the highest pressure rate on blitzes in the NFL this season (57.7%), per NextGen Stats, and were even worse against the Commanders on Sunday Night Football. The Commanders generated 10 pressures on 12 blitzes (83.3%), which is the second-highest pressure rate on blitzes by a team in the 2026 season.

The Eagles have now faced 109 blitzes this season and have given up 66 pressures — an outrageous rate of 60.6% The Eagles have also allowed a free rusher on 18.2% of their passing plays this season, the most of any team, according to Ryan Paganetti.

A big focus for the offense this week is to fix the communication errors that have plagued them through eight weeks. Sean Mannion also expressed his confidence in Kuper this week.

"I think Koop's been doing an outstanding job," Mannion said. "I know, like I said, everything ultimately starts with me and making sure I'm charting the right course for us in terms of our protection plan, in terms of how we're getting it taught, in terms of our install meetings and everything like that. But Chris is doing a great job. He's a tremendous teacher of the fundamentals. He's great with those guys in the room."

Kuper was not retained by the Vikings after the 2025 season and the Eagles hired him with the hope that his knowledge in this system would help the transition to Mannion. Nothing on offense has worked this year.

So there's no doubt that the pressure on Kuper has started to get ratcheted up in recent weeks as the Eagles' struggles continue. But the Eagles seem like they're sticking with him for now.

There are a lot of problems with the Eagles' offense this season, as Sirianni is happy to share.

"Again, we all have to do better," he said. "I think, again, we want to zero in on one thing. We're not coaching well enough right now and we're not playing well enough right now. I know we're speaking offensively and right now we're not doing all the things we need to do to win football games, and that falls on everyone's shoulders. It's not just one thing. We have to dig deep as coaches, as players, and again, find solutions, find answers."

#2915. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

#2912. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2911. Tech companies face mounting child safety lawsuits while pushing AI into schools

Tech companies have spent years telling parents they're working to make their products safer for children. But ongoing jury trials, settlements and related litigation against the likes of Meta, Google and Snap suggest that they're not doing enough.

The latest examples landed this week. Snap confidentially settled a case claiming that its technology caused severe psychological distress to two teenage users. Meta, TikTok and YouTube previously settled with the plaintiffs in the same suit. And Google was sued this week by the parents of a child who allegedly suffered a psychological crisis after chatting with the Gemini AI chatbot in Google Classroom.

Concern is intensifying as artificial intelligence companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations, and established internet giants pour money into developing advanced AI models while pushing them into the classroom. Products and features intended to maximize engagement can have detrimental effects on users, thus warranting "broader systemic changes in tech design, particularly when children are using them," said developmental psychologist Kate Blocker.

"I think it's so so important that children's safety is being considered when these products — AI, social media, et cetera — are being designed, and not after the fact as these problems emerge," said Blocker, the director of research and programs for the nonprofit Children and Screens.

Snap's settlement this week, which spared CEO Evan Spiegel from testifying in a Los Angeles court, continues a general trend of tech companies attempting to shield themselves from public scrutiny due to the various high-profile child-safety related trials this year, said Joseph McNally, the director of emerging litigation at McNicholas & McNicholas.

The case was one of several lawsuits related to the social media personal injury trial that Meta and YouTube lost earlier this year. More trials are slated to commence in March.

"I view this settlement and the other settlements in this space as an effort by tech companies to turn a corner, not only because I think that there were real legal challenges, but also the reputation of their products, the reputation of their companies has really been hit," said McNally, a former acting U.S. attorney for the Central District of California.

"The parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner," a Snap spokesperson said in a statement.

But there remains a federal case involving Meta, TikTok, Snap and YouTube and a litany of nationwide school districts, along with various state attorneys general suits. In August, Meta agreed to pay nearly $17 billion to settle with a group of state AGs in a child mental health case. Some AGs are still pursuing similar suits against Meta's rivals.

The social media lawsuits have helped inform parents and the public about how the industry designs products used by teenagers and children, McNally said.

"It's the technology arms race that continues," he said. "What is oftentimes missing is how you balance that participation in the arms race with utility and then product safety."

Google ran into more criticism this week for moving too fast. The New York Times reported on Tuesday that K-12 schools that don't allow use of Google's AI tools for students will lose access to search. Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that, "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

That coincided with the new lawsuit against Google centering on a teen's alleged suicidal crisis that the parents attributed to the company's Gemini AI. The plaintiff's attorneys allege that "despite Gemini's known risks, Google hastily integrated" Gemini into Chromebook computers for school children.

"We are reviewing the claims in the lawsuit and we take this very seriously," a Google spokesperson said in a statement. "We've worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it."

Google and its internet peers have for decades been able to rely on Section 230.

Passed in 1996, the federal legislation has protected social media companies from being sued over content posted by their users. That's why plaintiffs' attorneys and state AGs have been focused on alleged harms stemming from the design of the tech platforms as opposed to their content.

Some plaintiffs are using the same tactics against AI companies. OpenAI faces several lawsuits from state AGs and plaintiff attorneys, taking cues from the social media suits in alleging that the design of ChatGPT can pose risks to children.

The litigation could become a growing financial risk as the company plans for a potential blockbuster IPO. OpenAI competitor Anthropic, which is planning to go public this month, highlighted the issue in its prospectus, even though the Claude creator prohibits children from using its product.

"This is a clear indication that tech companies anticipate more litigation risks than they did a decade ago," said Michele Neitz, a professor at the University of San Francisco School of Law.

Blocker said that the highly personalized nature of current AI-powered chat apps makes it "a little bit unpredictable" to determine exactly what the apps will say to kids. She stressed the importance of testing and evaluating these products for children's developmental safety from the start, "not after harms start happening."

This collective litigation has helped educate the public on the potential pitfalls of poor product design choices, Blocker said. And it could be a catalyzing force for change in the tech industry.

"Certainly there's a hope that this will motivate real systemic change, both from a regulation standpoint and internal company decision-making standpoint," Blocker said.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

WATCH: AI challenge is lack of compute in the marketplace.

#2910. Eagles snap counts: Time to see more of Eli Stowers

Eagles snap counts: Time to see more of Eli Stowers  originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles have lost six games in a row and you're probably tired of watching Johnny Mundt and Grant Calcaterra play football.

That's probably a big harsh but the Eagles have now lost six games in a row and they're simply not going to recover this season. It's time to see what some younger players can do and Eli Stowers is right at the top of that list.

Here's how the Eagles' tight end snaps broke down in Sunday night's 27-10 loss to the Commanders:

Dallas Goedert: 55 snaps (80%)Johnny Mundt: 13 snaps (19%)Eli Stowers: 13 snaps (19%)Grant Calcaterra: 12 snaps (19%)

The Eagles signed Mundt to a one-year deal this offseason with the promise that he would be an ideal blocking tight end. Howie Roseman talked about needing to find that element for the tight end room and Mundt was supposed to be that guy. It hasn't worked. So even though Stowers doesn't fit that mold — he's more of an athletic pass catcher — it's time to see more of him.

In his second NFL game, Stowers caught his first NFL pass. It was a 7-yarder in the third quarter to move the chains on a 2nd-6. He also caught a couple passes in the final moments of the fourth quarter to give him 3 for 53 against the Commanders. In a season like this it becomes important to develop young talent. Stowers had 12 snaps in his debut and 13 in his second game; the Eagles should continue to increase his playing time.

• Tanner McKee and his entire offensive line played all 69 snaps against the Commanders. McKee was under pressure all night and was sacked five times. The blitz kept getting home.

• Without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks, the Eagles were obviously short-handed at receiver. Here's how the snaps broke down: Darius Cooper – 66 snaps (96%), Makai Lemon – 65 snaps (94%), Hollywood Brown – 32 snaps (46%), Elijah Moore – 18 snaps (26%). Lemon did some good things with 6 catches on 9 targets for 57 yards.

• Saquon Barkley played through hamstring and back injuries and got a total of 22 snaps (32%). He was bottled up way more in this game than he was a week ago against Dallas. Elijah Mitchell led the running back room with 39 snaps (57%) and Jaydon Blue got on the field for 8 snaps (12%).

• Willie Lampkin and Fred Johnson were on the field for that Barkley direct-snap touchdown in the first quarter. Lampkin lined up as a lead blocker.

• Cooper DeJean, Zack Baun and Quinyon Mitchell each played all 60 defensive snaps. Drew Mukuba (59) and Riq Woolen (58) were right behind them. It was another forgettable performance by the defense but at least Mukuba made a couple plays. He forced a fumble and had an interception that was negated because of a penalty.

• Without Jihaad Campbell (hamstring/quad), Jeremiah Trotter Jr. played 54 of 60 snaps (90%) and led the Eagles in tackles with 10.

• The DT rotation: Jalen Carter – 49 snaps (82%), Jordan Davis – 37 snaps (62%), Moro Ojomo – 36 snaps (60%), Byron Young – 15 snaps (25%), Ty Robinson – 9 snaps (15%).

• The edge rotation: Nolan Smith – 47 snaps (78%), Jonathan Greenard – 28 snaps (47%), Arnold Ebiketie – 24 snaps (40%), Jalyx Hunt – 21 snaps (35%). Smith returned from a concussion and at least provided some juice.

• Kenny Moore played 29 snaps but got banged up in the game. Marcus Epps ended up playing 13 snaps.

#2909. Grant Nelson leads Long Island with double-double but Nets fall to Greensboro

The Long Island Nets had several individual performances worth recognizing in Friday's G League opener and after all development is the point, but they ultimately couldn't do enough to overcome the Greensboro Swarm, falling 121-111 at Novant Health Fieldhouse.

The game was close throughout with 12 lead changes and three ties. After going down 60-55 at the half, Long Island was still down only four, 87-83 at the end of the third, the Swarm took over, outscoring the Nets, 34-28.

It was a frustrating result for Long Island, considering the contributions it received from multiple players, whether on assignment from Brooklyn, two-ways or Exhibit 10s. Arguably, the biggest positive came from two-way Grant Nelson, who delivered an impressive double-double and established himself as the team's leading performer … at least for opening night.

The 24-year-old 7-footer finished with 18 points and 13 rebounds in 27 minutes. The big man was active throughout his time on the floor, shooting 8-of-16 from the field and collecting six offensive rebounds. His ability to create additional possessions off the glass gave the Nets an important presence inside. The only negative for the Alabama product was his failure to hit on any of his five 3-pointers.

Nelson added two assists, one steal, and one block for a solid all-around performance. In a game that ended in a 10-point defeat, Nelson's performance was the clearest bright spot for the Nets.

Nelson's performance is an especially encouraging first step considering how limited his opportunities were last season. He appeared in just four games with Brooklyn, spending much of his time trying to establish himself despite knee issues that he says are now resolved after a summer of rehab.

There were also positive performances from two of the Nets 2026 picks.

Joshua Jefferson, taken with the 28th pick, had a solid showing with 15 points, six rebounds, three assists and only one turnover. The Iowa State product shot 5-of-9 from the field and connected on three of his seven attempts from beyond the arc. A 6'9", 240-pound point forward, he was selected by Brooklyn primarily because of his versatility which includes an ability to score from the perimeter while helping on the glass.

Tyler Bilodeau, the 6'9" sharpshooter taken at No. 43 in June, also provided a valuable offensive contribution, finishing with 13 points, four rebounds, and two assists. He also recorded five steals, giving Long Island an impact beyond his scoring. Bilodeau's five steals marked the highest number of thefts ever by a Long Island Nets player in a NBA G League debut.

The 2026 second-round selection out of UCLA, who like Nelson is on a two-way contract, shot 5-of-10 from the field and 3-of-7 from 3-point range.

On the other hand, Drake Powell, one of two members of the Flatbush 5 assigned to Long Island, had a difficult night offensively. The second year player finished with six points on 2-of-6 shooting, missing all three of his attempts from beyond the arc. He grabbed four rebounds and recorded three assists. He did record a block and a steal but the Nets needed more production from him. It was a game where his shot simply wasn't falling.

Ben Saraf, the other Brooklyn Nets player on assignment, also struggled to find his shooting touch. The 6'6" 20-year-old finished with eight points on 3-of-14 shooting from the field and just 1-of-8 from 3-point range. He had opportunities to make an impact as a scorer but couldn't get enough shots to fall. He also finished with three turnovers in 32 minutes.

There was at least one positive to take away from Saraf's performance: his playmaking. The young guard recorded a team-high seven assists, showing his ability to create opportunities. Defensively, he also added two steals. Saraf, a 2025 first-round pick who came to Brooklyn from German club Ratiopharm Ulm, is still adjusting to the NBA system and the professional game in North America.

Beyond the draft picks and two ways, there were some encouraging signs among the guys hoping to move up the G League ladder.

Isaiah Wong is the most experienced Long Island player, having played more than 20 games in the NBA as well as a season in the Spanish league. The 6'4" guard finished with 16 points, six steals, two assists and one rebound in 31 minutes. He shot an efficient 7-of-12 from the field and 2-of-4 from three-point range.

Dion Brown was another bright spot off the bench, recording 15 points in just 16 minutes. The St. Louis product shot 6-of-9 from the field and 3-of-5 from beyond the arc. Brown went undrafted in the 2026 NBA Draft but played well in the Summer League.

One of the most encouraging performances came from Dain Dainja, who made an immediate impression in his first game with the team. Coming off the bench, the 6'9" big with the gargantuan 7'7" wingspan finished with 16 points, six rebounds and three blocks in 21 minutes. He was efficient offensively, shooting 6-of-10 from the field.

Long Island won't have to wait long for an opportunity to respond . The Nets will remain in Greensboro for a rematch against the Swarm on Saturday, November 7, continuing the two-game opening-weekend series at Novant Health Fieldhouse.

After allowing 121 points in the opener, Long Island will look to make defensive adjustments and find a way to slow down Greensboro while building on the positives from Nelson's double-double and the contributions from its bench.

Boxscore: Greensboro Swarm 121, Long Island Nets 111 – G League

#2908. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.
